Keep\nthe scalar body and add a kernel in front of it:\n\n```cpp\n#include \"../dsp/simd.hpp\"\n\nnamespace kernels {\n#if CEDAR_SIMD_X86\nCEDAR_TARGET_AVX2 inline void mul_avx2(const float* a, const float* b, float* out) noexcept {\n    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < BLOCK_SIZE; i += 8)\n        _mm256_store_ps(out + i, _mm256_mul_ps(_mm256_load_ps(a + i), _mm256_load_ps(b + i)));\n}\n#endif\n}\n\ninline void op_mul(ExecutionContext& ctx, const Instruction& inst) {\n    // ... resolve a, b, out ...\n#if CEDAR_SIMD_X86\n    if (simd::has(simd::Isa::Avx2)) { kernels::mul_avx2(a, b, out); return; }\n#endif\n    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < BLOCK_SIZE; ++i) out[i] = a[i] * b[i];  // fallback stays\n}\n```\n\n- `CEDAR_TARGET_AVX2` is `[[gnu::target(\"avx2\")]]`; no compiler flags change.\n  FMA is **not** enabled (it would change rounding).\n- Buffers from `ctx.buffers->get()` are 32-byte aligned; `BLOCK_SIZE` is 128.\n- You may also add NEON (`CEDAR_SIMD_NEON`) or WASM SIMD128\n  (`CEDAR_SIMD_WASM`) paths; they are measured later on other machines, not\n  gated here.\n\n## Rules (enforced by the verifier \u2014 violations are rejected automatically)\n\n1. You may only edit: `cedar/include/cedar/opcodes/**`, `cedar/include/cedar/dsp/simd*.hpp`, `cedar/src/dsp/simd*.cpp`. Any other edit (benchmarks, tests,\n   experiments, CMake, scripts) rejects the whole attempt.\n2. **Never delete or change the scalar body.** It is the oracle and the\n   fallback; the verifier runs it with SIMD disabled and requires\n   bit-identical output to the original.\n3. Output must be **bit-identical** to the original for every opcode.\n   No approximations are allowed for these opcodes.\n4. The final verification also runs every opcode on **randomised stimuli you\n   cannot see** (different signals, levels down to denormals, parameter\n   trajectories). A kernel that only works for the stimuli in the benchmark\n   source fails there.\n5. No allocation, locks or syscalls in a kernel. CPU detection is already done\n   once at start-up; never detect features per block or per sample.\n6. The change must make at least one listed opcode **\u2265 5 % faster** than the\n   *current* version (outside measurement noise) and none slower.\n\n## Budget\n\nAt most **10 runs of the check command** and **30 minutes** for\nthis attempt. Every model in this experiment gets the same budget.\n\nYour tools are: read, write and edit files, search the repository, and run\nthe check command. **The check command is the only shell command allowed**\n(piping its output, e.g. through `tail`, is fine). You cannot run Python,\ncompilers or other scripts; do any numeric derivation (polynomial\ncoefficients, error bounds) yourself, and let the check measure it.\n\n## Method\n\n1. Read the code you need.\n2. **Before editing, state your hypothesis in one or two sentences:** what\n   bounds this opcode's speed (e.g. a serial feedback recurrence, a\n   transcendental call, memory bandwidth, branches, VM overhead) and the\n   single change you will make to address it.\n3.   "content": "     1\t# PRD: SIMD Autoresearch Loop\n     2\t\n     3\t> **Status: PARTIAL** \u2014 harness for phases 0\u20133 built and self-tested; the\n     4\t> model matrix (Phase 4) has not been run (2026-09-24). Executive Summary\n    11\t\n    12\tAn automated research loop that optimises hand-picked Cedar DSP opcodes with\n    13\tSIMD (SSE/AVX2, NEON, WASM SIMD128), runs the same loop across several models \u2014\n    14\tClaude, cheap hosted models, and local models \u2014 and logs both the **speedup\n    15\tachieved** and the **tokens spent** getting there.\n    16\t\n    17\tTwo outcomes, weighted equally:\n    18\t\n    19\t1. **Engine work.** Real, merge-quality SIMD kernels for opcodes that are hot\n    20\t   today, behind runtime dispatch, with the scalar path kept as fallback.\n    21\t2. **Talk artifact.** The closing section of *Compiling Music* at ADCx Gather:\n    22\t   a results table, a cost-per-speedup comparison across models, a screen\n    23\t   capture of the loop working, and at least one change the gates rejected.\n    24\t\n    25\tThe premise the talk rests on: the four-legged memory harness plus a\n    26\tbit-identity/null-test gate is what makes unattended optimisation safe. A model\n    27\tproposing SIMD kernels is only interesting because a wrong kernel cannot survive\n    28\tthe gates.\n    29\t\n    30\t**Decisions locked during design (2026-09-24):**\n    31\t\n    32\t- Candidate opcodes are **hand-picked and fixed** (\u00a73.1), so results are\n    33\t  comparable across models. The loop may nominate extra \"looks heavy\" targets,\n    34\t  but only the fixed set counts for the headline table.\n    35\t- Accepted changes are committed to a **scratch branch per run**; master is\n    36\t  never touched by the loop.\n    37\t- Gates: bit-identity where achievable, else a **null test within tolerance**;\n    38\t  plus existing test suites. The memory harness runs as a **final pass on the\n    39\t  winners only**, not per iteration.\n    40\t- Transcendental approximations (`tanh`, `tan`) **are allowed** with a\n    41\t  documented per-opcode exception, tolerance, and a human listening check.\n    42\t- **Runtime dispatch; no change to default build baselines.** WASM gets\n    43\t  `-msimd128` in its own preset.\n    44\t- Each (model, opcode) run gets a **fixed iteration count**, default 8.\n    45\t- One Python driver, many backends (\u00a74.2).\n    46\t\n    47\t---\n    48\t\n    49\t## 2. Problem Statement / Current State\n    50\t\n    51\t| Today | With this PRD |\n    52\t|---|---|\n    53\t| No CPU benchmark anywhere in the repo. `git grep -i benchmark` hits only prose in PRDs. | `cedar_bench` target: median ns/block per opcode, JSON output, stable enough to gate on. |\n    54\t| No SIMD. No `immintrin.h`, no `-march`, no `-msimd128`; every opcode is a scalar `for (i < BLOCK_SIZE)` loop. | SIMD kernels for the candidate set, selected at runtime, scalar fallback retained. |\n    55\t| Optimisation opportunities are noted in three PRDs and never measured (`prd-squelch-engine.md` \u00a7formant, `prd-stereo-native-opcodes.md` \u00a79.13 + OQ4). | Those exact targets are measured, attempted, and reported \u2014 including where SIMD does *not* win. |\n    56\t| No automation calls an LLM. No agent tooling in `.claude/` beyond one command and one skill. | `scripts/autoresearch/` drives models through an identical propose\u2192build\u2192verify\u2192bench\u2192accept loop. |\n    57\t| Correctness is guarded by unit tests, the memory harness, and human listening. | Same gates, plus an A/B null test of optimised vs baseline output through the `cedar_core` pybind harness. |\n    58\t\n    59\tGroundwork that already helps:\n    60\t\n    61\t- `cedar/include/cedar/vm/buffer_pool.hpp:39` \u2014 slabs are `alignas(32)`, rows are\n    62\t  contiguous `BLOCK_SIZE` floats. AVX-ready today.\n    63\t- `BLOCK_SIZE = 128` is a clean multiple of every vector width in play.\n    64\t- `cedar/bindings/bindings.cpp` \u2192 `cedar_core` pybind module: the fastest way to\n    65\t  drive one opcode with fixed input and capture output for A/B comparison.\n    66\t- `cedar/tests/test_zero_alloc.cpp`, `scripts/memory/run_all.sh`: the safety net\n    67\t  the talk's argument depends on.\n    68\t- `scripts/cedar-size-report.sh` is the structural template for \"measure across\n    69\t  configs, emit markdown, compare to baseline\".\n    70\t\n    71\t---\n    72\t\n    73\t## 3. Goals and Non-Goals\n    74\t\n    75\t### 3.1 Goals\n    76\t\n    77\t- **G1.** A per-opcode microbenchmark whose run-to-run noise is small enough that\n    78\t  a claimed \u226510 % speedup is real (\u00a76.1 defines the stability requirement).\n    79\t- **G2.** An autoresearch loop that, unattended, produces accepted SIMD kernels\n    80\t  for these candidates:\n    81\t\n    82\t  | # | Opcode | File | Shape | Prior estimate |\n    83\t  |---|---|---|---|---|\n    84\t  | C1 | `op_mul` / `op_add` / `op_sub` | `cedar/include/cedar/opcodes/arithmetic.hpp` | Pure elementwise, stateless, aligned | Control case; near-linear in lane count |\n    85\t  | C2 | `op_distort_tanh`, `op_distort_soft` | `cedar/include/cedar/opcodes/distortion.hpp` | Stateless waveshaper, loop-invariant branches | `soft()` exact; `tanh` needs approximation |\n    86\t  | C3 | `op_filter_formant` | `cedar/include/cedar/opcodes/filters.hpp:439` | 3 serial BPFs \u2192 4 lanes across *filters* | `prd-squelch-engine.md`: \"roughly halve\" |\n    87\t  | C4 | `op_filter_svf_lp/_hp/_bp` | `cedar/include/cedar/opcodes/filters.hpp:48` | Feedback-serial in time; only the 2-channel inner loop fuses | `prd-stereo-native-opcodes.md` OQ4; may be a loss |\n    88\t  | C5 | Opportunistic | any | Whatever the profiling step ranks as heavy | Reported separately from C1\u2013C4 |\n    89\t\n    90\t- **G3.** The same loop run across the model matrix (\u00a74.1) with identical\n    91\t  prompts, identical gates and identical iteration budget.\n    92\t- **G4.** Per-iteration cost logging (tokens in/out, cached, USD, wall clock) and\n    93\t  a per-run summary, in JSONL, regenerable into the talk's table and chart.\n    94\t- **G5.** Measurements on Linux x86-64 (gate), Apple Silicon over ssh (NEON),\n    95\t  and WASM SIMD128 (headless).\n    96\t- **G6.** Honest reporting of negative results. An opcode where SIMD loses, or a\n    97\t  model that never produces an accepted change, is a finding, not a failure to\n    98\t  hide. Budgets and tolerances are never loosened to make a run look better.\n    99\t\n   100\t### 3.2 Non-Goals\n   101\t\n   102\t- **Windows.** Dropped from the matrix for this round (was considered; the ssh\n   103\t  setup cost does not fit before 2 Oct). Future PRD.\n   104\t- **Vectorising the VM dispatch loop itself**, block-size changes, or\n   105\t  multithreaded graph execution. Opcode bodies only.\n   106\t- **Raising the default x86 baseline** (`-march=x86-64-v2/v3`). Runtime dispatch\n   107\t  instead; revisit in a future PRD if the dispatch overhead proves material.\n   108\t- **Auto-merging to master.** Scratch branches only; a human merges.\n   109\t- **Replacing the existing Python experiments** as the DSP quality oracle. They\n   110\t  stay; the loop adds an A/B null test on top.\n   111\t- **A general-purpose agent framework.** The driver does one job.\n   112\t\n   113\t---\n   114\t\n   115\t## 4. Experiment Design\n   116\t\n   117\t### 4.1 Model matrix\n   118\t\n   119\t| Tier | Model | Backend | Cost source |\n   120\t|---|---|---|---|\n   121\t| Reference | Claude Opus 5 | Claude Code headless (`claude -p`) | CLI usage JSON |\n   122\t| Hosted cheap | Claude Sonnet 5 | Claude Code headless | CLI usage JSON |\n   123\t| Hosted cheap (3rd party) | One GLM, one DeepSeek, one Qwen \u2014 coder tiers **[exact ids confirmed against the OpenRouter catalogue in Phase 3]** | OpenRouter API | `usage` field in response |\n   124\t| Local | One quantized coder model sized to the local GPU **[model chosen in Phase 3 from what is installed]** | LocalAI (Docker, `localhost:8069`) | tokens + GPU seconds, USD = 0 |\n   125\t\n   126\tEvery backend receives the **same prompt template**, the same repo state, the\n   127\tsame tool surface (read files, write files under the allowlist, run the verify\n   128\tscript) and the same iteration budget. Anything a backend cannot do (e.g. no\n   129\ttool calling) is recorded as a capability gap rather than papered over with a\n   130\tdifferent harness.\n   131\t\n   132\t### 4.2 The loop\n   133\t\n   134\t```\n   135\t                    \u250c\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500 run: (model, opcode), N=8 iterations \u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2510\n   136\t                    \u2502                                                                      \u2502\n   137\t  baseline bench \u2500\u2500\u25ba\u2502  1. context: opcode source + baseline ns/block + prior attempts       \u2502\n   138\t  (pinned, \u00a76.1)    \u2502  2. model proposes a patch (opcodes/ + simd/ files only)              \u2502\n   139\t                    \u2502  3. verify.sh:                                                        \u2502\n   140\t                    \u2502       a. build (scalar baseline build is cached)                       \u2502\n   141\t                    \u2502       b. A/B null test through cedar_core: bit-exact? else \u2264 tol       \u2502\n   142\t                    \u2502       c. cedar_tests + akkado_tests + [zero_alloc]                     \u2502\n   143\t                    \u2502       d. that opcode's experiments/test_op_*.py                         \u2502\n   144\t                    \u2502  4. bench: median ns/block, N reps, same machine, same governor        \u2502\n   145\t                    \u2502  5. verdict: accept (all gates green AND faster) | reject (+reason)    \u2502\n   146\t                    \u2502  6. log iteration JSONL (tokens, cost, wall clock, verdict, speedup)   \u2502\n   147\t                    \u2502  7. accepted \u2192 commit to run branch; rejected \u2192 revert, feed reason    \u2502\n   148\t                    \u2514\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2518\n   149\t                                                \u2502\n   150\t                            winners of all runs \u25bc\n   151\t                    final pass: memory harness (sanitizer + drift fuzz) + human listening\n   152\t```\n   153\t\n   154\t**Iteration budget:** fixed `N = 8` proposals per (model, opcode), regardless of\n   155\tcost. Token spend is *measured*, not capped \u2014 that is the comparison.\n   156\t\n   157\t### 4.3 Gates\n   158\t\n   159\t| Gate | Tool | Pass condition |\n   160\t|---|---|---|\n   161\t| Build | `cmake --build build/simd` | Compiles clean, warnings-as-errors as configured |\n   162\t| Output equality | A/B through `cedar_core`, fixed stimuli (seeded noise, sine sweep, impulse, ramp) | `np.array_equal` (bit-identical) **or**, where an approximation is declared, peak null-test error \u2264 **\u2212100 dBFS** and no new NaN/Inf/click per `artifact_metrics.scan_sanity` |\n   163\t| Unit suites | `cedar_tests`, `akkado_tests` | All green |\n   164\t| Zero-alloc | `cedar_tests \"[zero_alloc]\"` | No allocation on the audio path |\n   165\t| Opcode experiment | `experiments/test_op_<name>.py` | Passes its own assertions |\n   166\t| Speed | `cedar_bench` | Median ns/block improves by \u2265 **5 %** over baseline, outside the noise band (\u00a76.1) |\n   167\t| **Winners only** | `scripts/memory/run_all.sh`, then human listening to the rendered WAV | Green; no audible difference |\n   168\t\n   169\tA patch that declares an approximation must say so in a `// simd: approx` comment\n   170\tnaming the function replaced and the measured null-test floor. Approximations\n   171\twithout that declaration are rejected even if they pass the tolerance.\n   172\t\n   173\t### 4.4 Platforms\n   174\t\n   175\t| Platform | Role | How |\n   176\t|---|---|---|\n   177\t| Linux x86-64 (this machine) | **Gate.** Accept/reject decisions happen here. | Native build, AVX2 + SSE2 paths |\n   178\t| Apple Silicon (ssh) **[OPEN QUESTION: host + toolchain]** | Reported. NEON result for the portable-wrapper claim. | Remote build + bench of accepted kernels |\n   179\t| WASM SIMD128 | Reported. Matters most for nkido's actual users. | `wasm` preset + `-msimd128`, benched headless in node |\n   180\t\n   181\tRemote and WASM legs run **after** acceptance, on the winners, not inside the\n   182\tper-iteration loop.\n   183\t\n   184\t---\n   185\t\n   186\t## 5. Architecture\n   187\t\n   188\t### 5.1 Runtime dispatch\n   189\t\n   190\t```cpp\n   191\t// cedar/include/cedar/dsp/simd.hpp  (new)\n   192\tnamespace cedar::simd {\n   193\tenum class Isa { Scalar, Sse2, Avx2, Neon, Wasm128 };\n   194\tIsa active();              // resolved once at static-init, never in the audio path\n   195\tbool has(Isa) noexcept;\n   196\t}\n   197\t```\n   198\t\n   199\tEach optimised opcode keeps its scalar body and adds one or more kernels:\n   200\t\n   201\t```cpp\n   202\t// cedar/include/cedar/opcodes/arithmetic.hpp\n   203\t[[gnu::always_inline]] inline void op_mul(ExecutionContext& ctx, const Instruction& inst) {\n   204\t    // ... resolve a, b, out ...\n   205\t#if CEDAR_SIMD_X86\n   206\t    if (simd::has(simd::Isa::Avx2)) { kernels::mul_avx2(a, b, out); return; }\n   207\t#endif\n   208\t    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < BLOCK_SIZE; ++i) out[i] = a[i] * b[i];   // fallback stays\n   209\t}\n   210\t```\n   211\t\n   212\tConstraints the loop must respect:\n   213\t\n   214\t- The scalar body is **never deleted** \u2014 it is the oracle and the fallback.\n   215\t- No allocation, no syscalls, no locks in a kernel (the zero-alloc trap enforces).\n   216\t- CPU feature detection runs once at static-init; never per block, never per sample.\n   217\t- Buffers are already 32-byte aligned; kernels may assume it and must assert it\n   218\t  in debug builds.\n   219\t\n   220\t### 5.2 Repository layout (new)\n   221\t\n   222\t```\n   223\tscripts/autoresearch/\n   224\t  run.py              # driver: (model, opcode) \u2192 N iterations \u2192 JSONL\n   225\t  backends/\n   226\t    claude_code.py    # claude -p, parses usage JSON\n   227\t    openrouter.py     # GLM / DeepSeek, reads usage field\n   228\t    local_openai.py   # LocalAI / Ollama, OpenAI-compatible\n   229\t  verify.sh           # build + all gates \u2192 verdict JSON on stdout\n   230\t  bench.sh            # wraps cedar_bench, medians, noise band\n   231\t  report.py           # JSONL \u2192 markdown table + chart PNG\n   232\t  prompts/\n   233\t    optimize_opcode.md\n   234\t  runs/               # gitignored; one dir per run\n   235\tcedar/benchmarks/\n   236\t  bench_opcodes.cpp   # cedar_bench target\n   237\t```\n   238\t\n   239\t### 5.3 Benchmark harness\n   240\t\n   241\t`cedar_bench` is a plain C++ target \u2014 no new dependency, per the repo's\n   242\tzero-dependency habit:\n   243\t\n   244\t```\n   245\t./build/release/bin/cedar_bench --opcode op_mul --reps 2000 --warmup 200 --json\n   246\t{\"opcode\":\"op_mul\",\"ns_per_block_median\":41.2,\"ns_p10\":40.7,\"ns_p90\":42.9,\"reps\":2000,\n   247\t \"isa\":\"avx2\",\"cpu\":\"...\",\"governor\":\"performance\"}\n   248\t```\n   249\t\n   250\t- Drives one opcode over a fixed program through the VM, steady state.\n   251\t- Reports median and p10/p90; the **noise band** is `(p90 \u2212 p10) / median` from a\n   252\t  baseline-vs-baseline run. A speedup inside that band is not a speedup.\n   253\t- Records CPU model and scaling governor; refuses to gate (warns and marks the\n   254\t  run `unstable`) if the governor is not `performance`.\n   255\t\n   256\t### 5.4 Log schema\n   257\t\n   258\tPer iteration, appended to `runs/<run-id>/iterations.jsonl`:\n   259\t\n   260\t```json\n   261\t{\"run_id\":\"2026-09-28T14:03Z_opus5_op_distort_tanh\",\"iteration\":3,\n   262\t \"model\":\"claude-opus-5\",\"provider\":\"claude-code\",\n   263\t \"opcode\":\"op_distort_tanh\",\"isa_target\":\"avx2\",\n   264\t \"tokens\":{\"input\":18422,\"output\":2811,\"cached\":12000},\n   265\t \"cost_usd\":0.41,\"wall_clock_s\":96.3,\"gpu_s\":null,\n   266\t \"verdict\":\"accept\",\"reject_reason\":null,\n   267\t \"gates\":{\"build\":\"pass\",\"equality\":\"approx\",\"null_test_dbfs\":-112.4,\n   268\t          \"unit\":\"pass\",\"zero_alloc\":\"pass\",\"experiment\":\"pass\"},\n   269\t \"bench\":{\"baseline_ns\":118.7,\"candidate_ns\":41.9,\"speedup\":2.83,\"noise_band\":0.031},\n   270\t \"patch_sha\":\"...\",\"branch\":\"autoresearch/opus5-distort-tanh\"}\n   271\t```\n   272\t\n   273\tPer run, `runs/<run-id>/summary.json`: totals (tokens, USD, wall clock, GPU\n   274\tseconds), best speedup, iteration index of first accept, accept/reject counts.\n   275\t\n   276\t### 5.5 Anti-cheat\n   277\t\n   278\tThe loop measures the thing it optimises, so the model must not be able to touch\n   279\tthe measurement. `verify.sh` enforces a **write allowlist**:\n   280\t\n   281\t- Writable: `cedar/include/cedar/opcodes/**`, `cedar/include/cedar/dsp/simd*.hpp`,\n   282\t  `cedar/src/dsp/simd*.cpp`.\n   283\t- Everything else \u2014 benchmarks, tests, experiments, budgets, CMake \u2014 is restored\n   284\t  from a pristine checkout before every verify. A patch touching them is an\n   285\t  automatic reject, logged as `reject_reason: \"out-of-allowlist edit\"`.\n   286\t\n   287\tThis is also a talk beat: the first thing you defend against is the optimiser\n   288\toptimising the scoreboard.\n   289\t\n   290\t---\n   291\t\n   292\t## 6. Edge Cases\n   293\t\n   294\t1. **Benchmark noise / thermal drift.** Long runs heat the machine and slow late\n   295\t   iterations. Mitigation: interleave baseline re-measurement every iteration\n   296\t   (speedup is always ratio-to-a-fresh-baseline, never to a number from an hour\n   297\t   ago); mark runs `unstable` when the fresh baseline drifts > 10 % from the\n   298\t   pinned one.\n   299\t2. **Model edits the benchmark or the tests.** Rejected by the allowlist (\u00a75.5).\n   300\t3. **Patch does not compile.** Counts as an iteration; compiler output is fed\n   301\t   back as the rejection reason.\n   302\t4. **Bit-identity impossible** (`tanh`, `tan`, `pow`, FP reassociation). Falls to\n   303\t   the tolerance path; requires the declared `// simd: approx` comment.\n   304\t5. **Approximation passes the null test but changes musical behaviour** \u2014 e.g. a\n   305\t   `tanh` approximation that alters a filter's self-oscillation. Caught by the\n   306\t   winners-only listening pass; if it fails there, the change is rejected even\n   307\t   though every automated gate was green.\n   308\t6. **SIMD is slower.** Expected for C4 (feedback-serial). Recorded as a result;\n   309\t   the loop stops nominating that shape after two consecutive losses.\n   310\t7. **Local model too weak to produce a valid patch in 8 iterations.** Recorded as\n   311\t   zero accepted changes with its token cost \u2014 that is a finding for the talk,\n   312\t   not a reason to give it extra iterations.\n   313\t8. **Local model context window too small** for the opcode file. Recorded as a\n   314\t   capability gap; the prompt may extract just the function body, and the fact\n   315\t   that this was needed is reported.\n   316\t9. **Accepted change breaks a different opcode.** Full `cedar_tests` runs every\n   317\t   iteration, so this fails the gate; if it escapes, the winners-only memory\n   318\t   harness pass is the backstop.\n   319\t10. **Two runs collide on the same files.** One git worktree per run; runs are\n   320\t    serialised on the benchmark machine anyway (concurrent benching is invalid).\n   321\t11. **Runaway spend.** Fixed iteration count bounds it, but the driver also hard-\n   322\t    stops a run at **$20**, logging the run as `budget-stopped`.\n   323\t12. **Denormals.** A kernel that flushes denormals where the scalar path does not\n   324\t    changes output. FTZ/DAZ state must be identical in both builds; the A/B test\n   325\t    is run with the engine's normal settings.\n   326\t\n   327\t---\n   328\t\n   329\t## 7. Impact Assessment\n   330\t\n   331\t| Component | Status | Notes |\n   332\t|---|---|---|\n   333\t| `cedar/include/cedar/opcodes/*` (candidates) | **Modified** | Kernels added; scalar bodies kept intact |\n   334\t| All other opcodes | **Stays** | Untouched this round |\n   335\t| `cedar/include/cedar/vm/buffer_pool.hpp` | **Stays** | Already 32-byte aligned |\n   336\t| VM dispatch (`cedar/src/vm/vm.cpp`) | **Stays** | No instrumentation added to the hot switch |\n   337\t| `CMakePresets.json`, `cmake/CompilerOptions.cmake` | **Modified** | New `simd` build option + WASM `-msimd128`; default baselines unchanged |\n   338\t| `cedar/benchmarks/` | **New** | `cedar_bench` target |\n   339"

Every seed is guaranteed one\n    78\t    // near-denormal noise segment (1e-35..1e-25) and one hot one (2-4x),\n    79\t    // at random positions, so that coverage never depends on luck.\n    80\t    void make_hidden(std::mt19937& rng) {\n    81\t        std::uniform_real_distribution<float> u(0.0f, 1.0f);\n    82\t        constexpr std::size_t seg = STIM_BLOCKS * BLOCK_SIZE / 8;\n    83\t        const auto tiny_seg = static_cast<std::size_t>(u(rng) * 8.0f) % 8;\n    84\t        const auto hot_seg = (tiny_seg + 1 + static_cast<std::size_t>(u(rng) * 7.0f) % 7) % 8;\n    85\t        for (std::size_t s = 0; s < 8; ++s) {\n    86\t            int kind = static_cast<int>(u(rng) * 4.0f);\n    87\t            float level = gain * std::pow(10.0f, -30.0f * u(rng) * u(rng)) * (1.0f + 3.0f * u(rng));\n    88\t            if (s == tiny_seg) { kind = 0; level = gain * std::pow(10.0f, -25.0f - 10.0f * u(rng)); }\n    89\t            if (s == hot_seg) { kind = 0; level = gain * (2.0f + 2.0f * u(rng)); }\n    90\t            float dc = level * (2.0f * u(rng) - 1.0f);\n    91\t            for (std::size_t k = s * seg; k < (s + 1) * seg; ++k) {\n    92\t                switch (kind) {\n    93\t                    case 0: noise[k] *= level; break;\n    94\t                    case 1: noise[k] = u(rng) < 0.002f ? level * (u(rng) < 0.5f ? -1.0f : 1.0f) : 0.0f; break;\n    95\t                    case 2: noise[k] = (k % 4096 == 0) ?   "content": "This is very informative. The key facts:\n\n- The PRD itself notes: hand-written AVX2 `op_mul` reached only **1.32\u00d7**, not 1.5\u00d7, because \"the scalar loop is already SSE-autovectorised and the op is L1-bound\".\n- The scalar loop is already auto-vectorised to SSE2 (128-bit) by the compiler. So going from SSE2 (4 floats) to AVX2 (8 floats) only gains ~1.32\u00d7, and the op is L1-bound.\n\nSo the current accepted AVX2 kernel (8-float) is already the right approach, and the gains observed (0.94x/1.09x/1.03x) are within noise. To get a reliable \u22655% improvement over *current*, I need something genuinely better.\n\nThe bottleneck: these are L1-cache-bound memory streaming ops. 128 floats = 512 bytes per buffer. 3 buffers = 1.5KB touched per block. At 48kHz per opcode-block.\n\nLet me check the num of reads/writes. Each op reads a (512B), b (512B), writes out (512B). Total 1.5KB per opcode instance. With 64 instances that's 96KB per block, but each opcode instance has its own buffers.\n\nThe fundamental limit is memory bandwidth/L1 throughput. The compiler auto-vectorized the scalar path to SSE2 already. So AVX2 (double width) halves the number of load/store instructions, which is the real win since the arithmetic is trivial.\n\nWhat can I do to improve? The current kernel already uses `_mm256_load_ps` (aligned loads) and `_mm256_store_ps` (aligned stores). \n\nOne idea: the loop over BLOCK_SIZE with `i += 8` is 16 iterations. The compiler may not fully unroll this. Full unrolling with 16 explicit statements could reduce loop overhead, but with `[[gnu::always_inline]]` and constant bounds, the compiler likely already unrolls.\n\nActually, the real issue I notice: attempt 2 tried non-temporal store and it was slower. Why? Because the output buffer is re-read by subsequent processing (the benchmark runs 64 instances but each writes to distinct output, and in real usage, output feeds next opcode which reads it back from L1). Non-temporal streaming store evicts the line, forcing a reload.\n\nLet me think about a more impactful optimization. The dispatch `if (simd::has(avx2))` is fine but there's per-call branch. Actually since these are tiny, maybe the branch prediction handles it.\n\nWhat genuinely could help: **process the two inputs with a single loop that reads both into registers and the result**. That's already happening.\n\nAnother angle: since `BLOCK_SIZE` is 128 and the kernel is already 8-wide, the actual work is 16 vadd/vsub/vmul + 16 loads\u00d72 + 16 stores. That's minimal.\n\nLet me reconsider. The gate requires \u22655% faster than *current* version, and none slower. Current: add 15.46, sub 13.38, mul 14.09. Note add is *slower* than original scalar (14.47). That's strange \u2014 add got slower, sub got faster, mul got faster slightly.\n\nThese numbers are all within the noise band (baselines.json shows noise ~0.0028-0.0055, i.e. <0.6%). So the \"current accepted\" is essentially indistinguishable from scalar. The task wants me to make at least one \u22655% faster.\n\nThe real opportunity: reduce the number of instructions / memory operations. The scalar loop is auto-vectorized to SSE (128-bit). AVX2 (256-bit) already halves it. What's left?\n\nIdea: The opcodes read `a` and `b` and write `out`. These are 3 separate 512-byte buffers, likely in different cache lines but all L1-resident (pool is contiguous). \n\nCould I use a wider approach? AVX2 max is 256-bit. There's no AVX-512 in the gate machine (only AVX2).\n\nActually, let me reconsider the dispatch overhead. `simd::has()` inlines to `detail::g_active` read + compare. That's fine.\n\nLet me look at what the check actually measures and whether there's a hint. Let me check verify.py and the check script.\n\n",

For a genuinely memory-bound op, the optimal is: issue all 32 loads (16 a-loads + 16 b-loads) back-to-back, then all 16 adds, then all 16 stores. This maximizes MLP. The OOO window on Alder Lake is ~500+ instructions, so it CAN do this if the code has no artificial serialization. Without `__restrict`, though, the compiler must keep stores ordered relative to subsequent loads (in case of aliasing). This serializes: store(out[0..7]) must complete before load(a[8..15]) is *issued*? No \u2014 loads can issue speculatively, but the compiler won't reorder the STORE past a subsequent LOAD if they might alias, forcing them in program order in the generated code.\n\nSo aliasing (no restrict) forces: load a[0], load b[0], add, store out[0], load a[8], load b[8], add, store out[8], ... serialized!\n\nThis is the REAL bottleneck! The compiler can't batch all loads because of potential aliasing between `out` and `a`/`b`. Each 8-lane chunk is: 2 loads \u2192 1 add \u2192 1 store, and the store is a \"barrier\" for the next loads (compiler can't move load a[8] before store out[0] because out might alias a).\n\nThis is exactly why `__restrict` would help \u2014 but it's UNSAFE due to in-place aliasing.\n\nBUT \u2014 there's a safe way to get the aliasing benefit: the in-place pattern only aliases `out` with `a` (not `b`, typically, since out=a means a is read-modify-written). Actually in the akkado accumulator, `out` aliases `a` (`a[i] = a[i] + b[i]`). So `b` is always a DIFFERENT buffer from `out`.\n\nCan I safely help the compiler? I could do the loads of BOTH a and b for all chunks, THEN the arithmetic+stores. But if out==a, reading a[8..15] before writing out[0..7] is correct (different lanes). The only issue is reading a[i] for the SAME lane after writing.\n\nActually, the aliasing concern is: within one 8-lane op, `out[i..i+7] = a[i..i+7] + b[i..i+7]`. If out==a, then we read a[i..i+7], add, write back to a[i..i+7]. The read must happen before the write for the SAME 8 lanes. Cross-lane (a[i+8] vs out[i]) is not an aliasing issue.\n\nSo actually, the compiler only needs to preserve ordering WITHIN each 8-lane chunk, not BETWEEN chunks. But the compiler's conservative analysis without restrict is typically: \"out could point anywhere in a, so a store to out[i] must be ordered before any load from a[j] for j > i\". This over-conservatively serializes.\n\nA safe way to break this: manually structure the code so all LOADS happen first (using local accumulators), then all arithmetic, then all stores. Like:\n\n```cpp\n__m256 av0 = _mm256_load_ps(a + 0), bv0 = _mm256_load_ps(b + 0);\n__m256 av1 = _mm256_load_ps(a + 8), bv1 = _mm256_load_ps(b + 8);\n... all 16 pairs ...\n__m256 r0 = _mm256_add_ps(av0, bv0);\n... all 16 ...\n_mm256_store_ps(out + 0, r0);\n... all 16 ...\n```\n\nThis explicitly does all loads before all stores. The compiler generates this as written (it won't reorder stores before loads because... wait, it COULD reorder since it doesn't know about aliasing, but the semantics I've written have loads before stores, and the compiler preserves that ORDER in the generated code UNLESS it proves no aliasing).\n\nHmm, actually the issue is the compiler might reorder my explicit stores before loads if it thinks they don't alias... but WITHOUT restrict it assumes they MIGHT alias, so it will KEEP my written order: all loads first, then stores. That's exactly what I want!\n\nWait, but the loads themselves: if out==a, doing `load a[0..127]` first (all into registers), THEN `store out[0..127]`, a read of all a first then write all out (=a) \u2014 this is CORRECT for element-wise add because each lane reads its input then writes its output, and if I load ALL lanes before storing ANY lane, there's no issue since store[out[i]] = a[i]+b[i] where a[i] was already loaded.\n\nYES! This is the safe way to get the MLP benefit of `__restrict` WITHOUT the danger. Because:\n- All loads of `a` and `b` happen first (into 32 registers).\n- All arithmetic happens.\n- All stores happen last.\n\nIf out==a, the stores write to a's locations AFTER all a's values are already in registers. Bit-identical and correct.\n\nThis lets the CPU overlap all 32 loads (MLP), all 16 adds, all 16 stores, instead of the serialized load\u2192add\u2192store\u2192load\u2192add\u2192store pattern the compiler generates when it must preserve aliasing order.\n\nThis is a genuinely better structure and should give a real speedup!\n\nLet me rewrite the kernels to separate loads, compute, stores. With 16 chunks \u00d7 (2 loads) = 32 loads, 16 arithmetic, 16 stores.\n\nFor readability and to let the compiler allocate registers, I'll write it with a loop that first does all loads into a local array... no, arrays would spill to stack. Prefetch won't help.\n\nBut wait \u2014 in a REAL scenario (first block, cold output), prefetching for write could help. Let me reconsider the numbers: 12ns for 128 floats. On a 4.8GHz core, 12ns \u2248 58 cycles. For 64 instructions (16 add + 32 load + 16 store), that's ~1.1 IPC. Modern cores do 4-wide, so we're at 27% issue rate. This means we're LATENCY-bound (waiting on L1 loads), not throughput-bound.\n\nL1 load latency is ~4-5 cycles. With 32 dependent-free loads, the OOO engine should hide latency well. But if the compiler serializes (aliasing), each 8-float chunk waits: load a (5cy) \u2192 load b (5cy, can overlap with a) \u2192 add \u2192 store. If not overlapping across chunks, 16 chunks \u00d7 ~5cy = 80 cycles. That matches ~12ns!\n\nSo the aliasing serialization IS the bottleneck, and my load-separation attempt SHOULD have helped... but it caused register spills (32 regs > 16) in the full-unroll, and the 8-vector chunk version also failed.\n\nWhy did the 8-vector chunk version (check 5) fail? It used 16 registers (8 a + 8 b) which is exactly 16, leaving 0 for the results r0-r7. So r0=OP(a0,b0) requires a destination register, but all 16 are live. The compiler must free a0,b0 first (they die after r0 computed), but it has to interleave compute into the load phase, potentially spilling.\n\nActually there are 16 architectural YMM but more importantly the compiler needs temps. With 8 a + 8 b live, computing r0=a0+b0 needs the compiler to overwrite a0 in-place or use a temp. It can compute r0 into a0's register (since a0 dies). But then it's serialized: can't compute r0 until... it can, a0 is ready after its load. So compute r0=a0+b0 in place of a0's reg, freeing b0 too. Then r1 in a1's reg... This should work without spilling, but it forces compute to happen right after loads, reducing the \"batch all loads\" benefit.\n\nActually the deeper issue: my macro used `const` for a0..a7, b0..b7 AND r0..r7, making 24 named consts. The compiler sees 8 a + 8 b (16) live, then r0..r7 (8) live with a/b dying. Peak should be 16. But GCC might have spilled anyway due to scheduling.\n\nThis is getting too deep into compiler specifics I can't observe.
